| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/start` | Start now, a job waiting for its `startWhen`. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/permission` | Answer a waiting permission. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode` | Change a job's mode. |
| `GET /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` | The sentence publishing would carry out. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` | Publish, approved on the Watch (below). |
| `POST /v1/notifications` | The Watch's push tokens (below). |
| `POST /v1/speech` | A piece of a held recording (below). |

### `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` on the Watch API

The Watch approves with a turn of the crown, after showing the runner's sentence. `{"action": "Push oby/fix-ab12cd to deskree-inc/oby and open a pull request into dev."}`: the signed request is the approval (fresh, once, from a paired Watch's key), and `action` must be the runner's own sentence exactly, else `403`. Then Publish runs as for `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` above, and returns `{"url": …}`.

### `POST /v1/notifications` on the Watch API

The Watch's own push tokens: `{"token": "5b0e9c…", "topic": "com.deskree.oby.watchkitapp", "environment": "production", "kind": "alert"}`.

- `kind` `"alert"` (the default) is the Watch app's device token. The runner sends it exactly the alerts and clearing pushes it sends the iPhone (`POST /v1/notifications` above), the same payload with the same `apns-collapse-id`, so the Watch shows each alert once.
- `kind` `"widget"` is the complications' WidgetKit push token. When the number of jobs that need you or are working changes, the runner sends `{"aps": {"content-changed": true}}` (`apns-push-type: widgets`, topic `<topic>.push-type.widgets`), and the complications fetch the jobs themselves. A newer widget token replaces the Watch's older one.
- It returns `{"push": true}` or `{"push": false}`, and `422`, as `POST /v1/notifications` does. Unpairing the Watch drops its tokens.

### `POST /v1/speech?request={id}&sequence={n}` on the Watch API

A piece of the recording the user is holding the talk button for: the body is mono signed 16-bit little-endian PCM at 16 kHz (`application/octet-stream`), up to 32,000 bytes (one second), `request` the hold's id (8 to 64 letters, digits or `-`) and `sequence` 0, 1, 2… in order.

The Watch API doesn't pass pieces on to the runner's speech service (`POST /v1/speech` on the tailnet API, above) yet: a well-formed piece is answered `501`, `{"error": "Speech-to-text isn't set up on the runner yet. Dictate instead."}`, and the Watch stops recording and offers the system's dictation. `400` without `request` and `sequence`, `422` for an empty, odd or too-long piece.

## What the runner never does

- **Push to main:** not for anyone, not even with an approval.
- **Force-push, except a rebase of its own branch:** only an `oby/` branch the runner made, only over the commit it pushed there last (`--force-with-lease`), never `main` or `dev` ([`GET /v1/jobs/{id}/publish`](#get-v1jobsidpublish)).
- **Merge, except into `dev` after the Mac check:** the runner squash-merges a pull request it opened only into `dev`, only when Oby's review approved it, the Mac check passed on the same commit and the secret scan is clean (Mac check, above). Never into `main`.
- **Publish what didn't pass:** only a job that ended `done` (its check passed, or it has none), with a clean secret scan, is pushed and gets a pull request; by hand, only with a fresh device signature. No comment goes out, but a review the owner approved (obid only: GitHub work, "Posting it"): Oby's own review of its own pull request and the Mac check's results on it are check runs (an Apple check posts nothing), and the `oby-failure` issues its post-mortems file in Oby's own repository (Engine/Failures.swift) are updated in place, all after the secret scan; no agent posts to GitHub, and no GitHub review, approval or comment goes on anyone else's work.
- **Research jobs never commit or publish.** Proposed issues are filed only after the user taps to file them.
- **Touch the user's credentials:** it never reads, stores or forwards agent credentials, and each tool signs in through its own login on the runner.
- **Read outside text as instructions:** text from GitHub or other people reaches agents only as quoted files.

## obid only

The Rust daemon `obid` (`core/`, docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) serves this whole API, so the apps keep working against it unchanged, and also the endpoints in this section. **The Swift runner is frozen and never implements them.** They are what Obi-to-Obi work and the end-to-end scenario (e2e/SCENARIO.md) need. JSON keys are camelCase as everywhere in this API; objects of the Obi-to-Obi protocol (envelopes, agent cards, action bodies) appear exactly as the protocol defines them, in snake_case (obi-ai-bot/obi-sphere, `spec/`). None of these endpoints is on the gateway's allowlist (Runner/Routes.swift): they are managed on the machine itself. A phone answers an Obi-to-Obi request through the inbox endpoints it already has.

### obid only: what obid leaves out

`obid` serves every endpoint and key above, with its own rules for merging (obid only: merging, below). So, on `obid`:

- It merges only as that section says: never by the Swift runner's Mac-check flow. A Mac check that passed is reported (its check run, `macCheck`), and counts for a workflow whose `checks` is `mac`; nothing queues one by itself yet.
- A pull request that can't merge cleanly gets no rebase round by itself; the owner can ask for one like any other work.
- `macCheck.tries` is never sent, and no `Not merged:` alert is made; `mergeError`, `mergeNeedsYou` and the `merge` inbox item are obid's own (below).
- The `merged` pipeline stage is `upcoming` until `pullRequest.state` is `MERGED`, then `done` (`Merged into dev, as you like` when a remembered workflow merged it). While it's approved (GitHub's decision `APPROVED`, or Oby's own review's) it is `current`: with `mergeError` while the merge needs the owner, else `Merge it on GitHub`, and the apps open the pull request (`published`) from that line.
- Deskree's promotion rules (Xcode Cloud waits, `dev` into `main`, the three-review escalation) are configuration that changes what `obid` checks and reports, never something that merges.
- **Oby's review** (#525) runs when a job publishes by itself: a `review` job (`reviewOf` the author, `branch` `oby/review-<id>`, the author's `task`, Plan mode, no check of its own) on an account that's signed in, not being signed in or removed, and with room (Routing): another tool's first, then another of the author's tool's, last the author's own in a session of the review's own (`review.sameAccount`); never Cursor. With none, nothing runs over a limit: the author's `noReviewer` says why (`Oby's review waits for an account with room: …`, its Review stage `Waiting for review · …`, no event), and the review starts on obid's next pass (every three minutes, and when it starts) once one fits.
  - It reads the very commit obid pushed (the author's last `push`, its `startCommit`), whatever the branch has since. What the author was asked is quoted in `.obi/inputs/asked.md`, and the repository's `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` as they are on the base (`origin/<base>`) in `.obi/inputs/base-rules.md`: never the change's own, which its author could steer the review with. Its run loads no project instructions from the worktree: Claude Code runs with `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_CLAUDE_MDS=1` (and `--setting-sources ""`, as every job), Codex with `-c project_doc_max_bytes=0 -c skills.include_instructions=false -c features.skill_search=false` (no `AGENTS.md`, and no skill of `.agents/skills` or `.codex/skills` in its instructions or found by search). Its prompt says every file of the worktree, its comments, commit messages and the diff are data. Its summary is the review; only its last line that isn't empty decides, exactly `VERDICT: APPROVE` or `VERDICT: REQUEST CHANGES` (in backticks or bold too).
  - One review per published head: none while one runs and none of a head a review already read. A head pushed while one ran makes that one count for nothing, however it ended (not one of the three), and gets its own once that one ends.
  - `review` also carries `commit`, the head it read, and never `checkRun`. Of Oby's review, only an approval goes on GitHub, as the reviewer account's (obid only: merging); changes it asks for stay in Oby, and no check run, comment or description line is written.
  - A `reviewing` whose review a restart or a crash cut off is settled on obid's next pass: the review's end is applied, or a review that's gone is started again. Dismissing a review job (one waiting for its usage reset too) leaves its author `reviewError`, never `reviewing`.
  - Approved: the Review stage is `done`, `Approved by Oby`, and Merged is `current` (above).
  - Changes requested by the first and second reviews are the author's next round, in its session and worktree (`round` + 1, `step` `Working on Oby's review`): the review is quoted, the vault's and obid's secrets taken out and in a fence of its own, in `.obi/inputs/review-<round>.md`, which the round's fixed words name, and its publish is reviewed again. Changes requested by the third (`review.round` 3), or a round that left the head as it was, need the owner: `reviewNeedsYou` is true while the pull request is open, the Review stage is `failed` (`Changes requested`, reason `Oby requested changes.`), and the orchestrator raises one `review_error` event. No fourth review starts by itself, and no Mac check is queued.
  - A review that ended `failed` or `interrupted` before its verdict (a login refused, as codex-cli 0.162.1's `workspace routing discovery unauthorized (401)`; the tool crashing) goes to the next reviewer that may take it, by the same order, never one whose review of that head ended so: once, by itself. When none can (none left, or the second ends so too), the job needs the owner: `reviewError` `Oby couldn't review #7: <why>`, `reviewNeedsYou` true while the pull request is open (until a new head is pushed), the Review stage `Waiting for review · …`, an alert (`Couldn't review: <title>`, below), and one `failed` inbox item (`<job>-review-<review job>`, its `text` the `reviewError`) with `Try again` alone: it runs the review again, with one more reviewer to fall back on, and a second give-up is one new item. Merging without Oby's review isn't offered: no workflow merges without a review (`oby` waits for Oby's own, `reviewer`, `teammate` and `person` for someone's on GitHub). Nothing loops: no event goes to the orchestrator for it, and its item is the owner's alone. The failed review job is dismissed (its end is its author's), so it asks no one itself; one the owner dismissed (waiting for its usage reset, say) isn't tried again. Internally the author keeps which reviews of its head failed (`review_failed`, never sent).
  - A review that was stopped, made commits or gave no verdict leaves `reviewError` (`Oby's review didn't finish: …`; the Review stage `Waiting for review · …`) and one `review_error` event; one waiting for its account's usage reset comes back by itself. The review job's own end is no event.
  - Every job that publishes by itself is reviewed, an untrusted turn's too. A job published by hand isn't: its Review stage reads `Pull request open · waiting for your review on GitHub` until GitHub has a decision.
- The Watch API's `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` isn't served (#454).

### obid only: merging (#853, #854)

Oby carries a task the whole way (AGENTS.md, the owner's decision of 2026-10-24): once its pull request is approved and green, it merges it the way the repository's workflow says, and asks the owner only when something goes wrong. This part is the merge itself and the rules it keeps (`crate::merge`), and the typed workflow it follows (obid only: memory, below).

**What the follower does** (every read of a job's open pull request: when `obid` starts, right after it publishes one, and every three minutes). Only for a `done`, published job that isn't dismissed, a review or research job, or another Obi's:

1. **Oby's own review is the review** (the owner's decision of 2026-10-24: "approve using my own GitHub account, just like Claude Code does"). GitHub never lets a pull request's author approve it, so for the owner's own pull requests Oby's own review of exactly the head obid pushed (`review`, while it's the pull request's head) is what an `oby` workflow (the default it proposes) waits on, and the owner's own `gh` merges it: no second GitHub account is needed, and nothing is posted on GitHub for it. obid has no GitHub app, so no `Oby review` check run is written either; the verdict is on the job (`review`). It counts only on the owner's own pull request: its author must be the owner's gh login (any case), else the owner hears it. Where GitHub's rules for the base require an approving review, the owner's own approval can't count: `reviewDecision` `REVIEW_REQUIRED` always means another person's is needed, whatever approvals obid sees (GitHub knows who may approve, code owners included); otherwise the most of its rulesets' `pull_request` rule and its classic protection's `required_approving_review_count` (`GET repos/{repo}/rules/branches/{base}` and `…/branches/{base}/protection/required_pull_request_reviews`) must be met by approvals from other people with write access, never one of Oby's reviewer accounts. Classic protection counts as 0 only for GitHub's `Branch not protected`, `Required pull request reviews not enabled` and the `Not Found` an owner without admin rights gets (then `reviewDecision` still says when it asks for a review); a rate limit, a 5xx, a timeout or an answer it can't read is unknown. Then a teammate's Oby is asked (below), on a workflow the owner taught Oby; when none can, the owner hears it once (`GitHub requires another person's approval here.`, and why). A repository Oby hasn't learned asks the owner first, once for that head (the owner's decision of 2026-10-25), unless they said yes to it before (a `review` memory: then a teammate's Oby is asked by itself, with no card): a `merge` card, `Needs a teammate: <title>`, `GitHub requires another person's approval on <repo>. Ask a teammate's Oby to review it, and remember that for this repository?`, with `Ask a teammate, and remember` and `Not now` (`id` `merge-teammate-<job>-<head>`). Out of an Obysphere it says so instead, with what to do (`GitHub requires another person's approval on <repo>, and Oby isn't in an Obysphere, so no teammate's Oby can review it. Invite a teammate who can push to <repo> to an Obysphere with you, or get their approval on GitHub yourself.`). Rules it can't read: it waits, asking nobody, unless GitHub refused in a way that won't pass by itself (a `401`, or a `403` that isn't a rate limit: an organization's SSO not authorized for the owner's login, a token without access); then the owner hears it once for that head, learned workflow or not, in GitHub's words (`Oby can't read this repository's merge rules (GitHub said: …).`), and the card settles once GitHub shows them. The reviewer account's own state matters only to a `reviewer` workflow: one signed in once and stale since never stalls any other. On a `reviewer` workflow, a reviewer login that isn't signed in any more, that gh can't read, or that is the owner's own waits on that account for good, so the owner hears it once for that head (`Oby's reviewer account needs signing in again, or signing out (…).`), settled once it's signed in again.
2. **The reviewer account, an option.** With a `reviewer` workflow and Oby's reviewer account signed in, once Oby's own review approved exactly the head obid pushed, on the owner's own pull request (its author is the owner's gh login), obid posts an approval as Oby's reviewer account (`POST …/pulls/{n}/reviews` with `event` `APPROVE` and `commit_id` that head, as the reviewer, `body` obid's fixed words, scanned first), once: none when the reviewer approved that head already, and never again on a head where someone dismissed its approval. It never posts changes requested: those stay in Oby and drive the author's rounds. Right before it's sent, the job is read again (still done, not let go, at that head), and a mark of what's being sent is saved (`sending`, never in the API: one per kind, the approval, the merge and the branch's deletion, so one never overwrites another); a restart in between reads GitHub on the next pass and settles each: an approval it finds there is listed, the pull request open or merged since (a merged or closed one is read again until its reviews read); a merge that landed is listed as who had it merged; a branch deletion is tried again (one gone counts) and listed. The post is in `outgoing` as `pullRequestApproval` (`id` the review's URL; the pull request's when it's listed after a restart). Where it can't be posted (the reviewer can't push, or someone dismissed its approval: `Someone dismissed Oby's approval of this commit on GitHub, so Oby won't approve it again: …`), the job needs the owner: `mergeError`, `mergeNeedsYou`, and one `merge` card. With no reviewer signed in, a `reviewer` workflow merges on Oby's own review, as an `oby` one does. Anywhere else (an `oby`, `teammate` or `person` workflow, or a repository Oby hasn't learned) nothing is ever posted as the reviewer: it can push, so GitHub's branch rules would count its approval where only a teammate's should.
3. **A teammate's approval.** With a `teammate` or `person` workflow, while the merge waits on an approval of the head from someone other than the owner and Oby's reviewer accounts (`merge::wanted_approval`; checks may still be running), or where GitHub's rules require one on a pull request Oby's own review approved (`merge::needs_another_person`), the follower asks a teammate's Oby over the Obysphere to review that head and approve it on GitHub as that teammate ([teammate approval](#obid-only-teammate-approval), `Link::ask_teammate`), on each read: one teammate at a time, never another after a review that asked for changes. While one has it, nothing waits on the owner; where none can (Oby isn't in an Obysphere, no teammate's Oby can approve it, or one's review asked for changes), one `merge` card with X5's words. The merge goes on once GitHub shows that approval.
4. **The merge.** With a workflow the owner taught Oby for the repository, when everything below holds, it merges (`mergedBy` `{"by": "memory", "memory": "<id>"}`, the memory's `used` + 1), with the same mark saved right before the merge is sent; a restart that finds the pull request merged lists the merge as that, and one still open tries again as anything does. Where Oby can't go on by itself, one `merge` card (`Not merged: <title>`, the reason as its text, `Dismiss`) and `mergeError`; what may pass by itself waits for the next read, with nothing to answer.
5. **A repository Oby hasn't learned** gets "Ready to merge?" once everything holds by the workflow it proposes (a squash into the pull request's base, approved and checked the way it was: Oby's own review of the head, else the reviewer account's approval or a person's; GitHub's checks or the Mac's): a `merge` card, `Ready to merge: <title>`, `<repo>#<n> is approved and its checks passed. I don't know how <repo> merges yet, so I left it open. main is untouched.`, with `Merge`, `Merge, and remember` and `Leave it open`, and `mergeNeedsYou` with that text until it's answered. A pull request into `main`, `master` or `trunk` gets none.

**When it's merged or closed.** Whoever merged or closed it, nothing waits on the owner any more: `mergeError` is null, `mergeNeedsYou` false, and the job's open `merge` cards are settled. One merged on GitHub (not by obid) gets `mergedBy` `{"by": "github", "at": "<mergedAt>"}`. While `mergeNeedsYou` is true the job needs the owner, so it can be dismissed.

**The `merge` card.** One per job, head and kind: "Ready to merge?" (`id` `merge-ready-<job>-<head>`), or one for each reason it needs the owner (`merge-why<hash of the reason>-<job>-<head>`), so a new reason, like a reviewer signed in since, replaces the card before. A card the follower doesn't raise again is settled, with no answer: a new head, a reason gone (checks running again, GitHub working out whether it merges, the reviewer signed out), a merged or closed pull request, a job that runs again or is dismissed; it's offered again when what it asked about comes back. One the owner answered isn't, and nothing waits on them about that head and reason (`mergeNeedsYou` false): `Leave it open`, `Dismiss`, or done, which counts as the card's way of leaving it. Its answers go only through `POST /v1/inbox/{id}/answer`: the brain (`answer_inbox`, `oby_answer`) and the event orchestrator never answer one, and no other Obi can. `Merge` reads GitHub again; the pull request must still be at that head and hold by the card's workflow (or one the owner taught since), and it merges, `mergedBy` `{"by": "owner"}`. `Merge, and remember` does that, then keeps the workflow: the owner's yes to the card's "Can I remember that?", saved as Remember saves it (obid only: memory). Either claims the card first, so a second tap merges nothing (`409`); one that didn't merge answers `409` with why and leaves it open. `Ask a teammate, and remember` is the owner's yes to its "Can I remember that?", about who reviews, never how it merges: obid checks the job is still one it carries at the head it pushed, reads GitHub again (still open, still at that head), and keeps a `review` memory for the repository (obid only: memory; `from` `You asked a teammate's Oby to review <repo>#<n>`), or, where the owner taught it a workflow since, changes only that workflow's `review` to `teammate`; then it asks a teammate's Oby right away (below): the card is done, and the answer's `note` is what came of the ask (`Asked ernesto's Oby to review it and approve it on GitHub.`, or why none can, which the follower's next card says too). It's claimed the same way; one that couldn't remember or read answers `409` (`Nothing was asked: …`) and leaves it open. Every later pull request there that needs another person's approval goes to a teammate's Oby by itself, with no card. Nothing merges by it: once the teammate's approval shows, "Ready to merge?" asks as on any repository Oby hasn't learned, until the owner's `Merge, and remember` teaches it how. `Not now` or done settle it and nothing is sent: that head waits quietly. Merged, but the workflow couldn't be kept: the card is done, and the answer's `note` says so (`Merged, but Oby couldn't remember how: it asks again next time.`). `Leave it open` (on "Ready to merge?") or done settle it and nothing is sent: that head isn't asked about again; `Dismiss` (on a card saying why) or done settle that one.

**When Oby may merge.** Only when every one of these holds; the merge checks them all itself, on what GitHub says right then, whoever asks:

- The repository has a workflow: the owner's `workflow` memory (only the owner's Remember saves one), or the owner's own tap naming the base. Free text never drives a merge.
- The pull request's base is the one the workflow names. Never `main`, `master` or `trunk`, whatever a workflow says (one into them can't be saved either); the repository's default branch only when the workflow names it (this repository's default is `dev`).
- It's the job's own: the job's `oby/` branch, in the repository itself (never a fork's), published by obid, its head the commit obid pushed last (`outgoing`) with nothing unpublished, and the secret scan of that push clean (`scan`). The job is `done`, not dismissed, and the owner's own: never another Obi's (`obi`). A job that read something Oby didn't write merges as any other: it publishes by itself (the owner's decision of 2026-10-24), and its merge waits on the same review.
- The one the workflow's `review` names approved that very head. For `oby` (and `reviewer` with no reviewer account signed in): Oby's own review, `approved`, of exactly that head as obid pushed it, and, where GitHub's rules require approving reviews, that many from other people (below). For the others: a reviewer's latest review (above) `APPROVED` with `commit_id` the head, from someone with write access (`author_association` `OWNER`, `MEMBER` or `COLLABORATOR`), a person's (`user.type` `User`; never a bot's), and never the pull request's author's. An author GitHub doesn't name stops the merge. For `reviewer`, only Oby's reviewer account's approval counts, and none while it isn't signed in; for `teammate` and `person`, anyone's but the owner's (its author) and any reviewer account's Oby has ever used, signed in or not (below). When Oby can't read which reviewer accounts it used, nothing merges. Anyone's latest review asking for changes stops it, whoever wrote it, and so does GitHub's `reviewDecision` `CHANGES_REQUESTED` or `REVIEW_REQUIRED`.
- The checks the workflow names passed on that head: `mac`, a Mac check (`macCheck`) `passed` with that `head`; `github`, GitHub's checks on it, at least one, every one finished `SUCCESS`, `NEUTRAL` or `SKIPPED`. Whatever the workflow names, none of GitHub's checks on the head may be failing or pending. There's no workflow without a check: nothing merges without one that passed on that very commit.
- GitHub says it merges cleanly: `mergeable` `MERGEABLE` and `mergeStateStatus` `CLEAN` or `HAS_HOOKS`; with `rebaseFirst`, it isn't behind its base either (`GET …/compare/<base>...<head>`, `behind_by` 0).
- The commit title and message Oby writes pass the secret scan (below). With `merge` or `rebase`, whose commits land as they are, no commit says it closes an issue (`Not merged: a commit says it closes an issue, and a rebase would land it as it is.`), and the pull request has fewer commits than the 250 GitHub lists, so none goes unread.

**The merge.** With obid's own `gh`, never inside a job: `gh pr view` (the follower's fields), the reviews and `GET repos/{repo}/pulls/{n}` once more (the reviews again right before the merge, so a change request made meanwhile stops it), the second to check it's still open, into the same base, at the same head, from the job's branch in the repository itself, and not conflicting. For `squash` and `merge`, Oby writes the commit title and message itself, as GitHub does a squash's by default: the pull request's title and ` (#n)`, then one commit's body, or each commit's message as a bullet (`GET …/pulls/{n}/commits`, every page), with every closing keyword (`Closes #116`, `fixes o/r#7`) turned into `Part of`, so nothing from the description lands in the base. Both go through the secret scan (the publish gate's, with the vault as it is then): a finding, or a scan that can't run, sends nothing (`Not merged: possible secret in the squash commit's message.`, never the value). Then `gh api -X PUT repos/{repo}/pulls/{n}/merge --input <file>` with `merge_method` (the workflow's `method`), `sha` (the head it checked, so GitHub refuses if the head moved since) and, but for `rebase`, `commit_title` and `commit_message`; the file is 0600 in `home/tmp/merge-<job id>/`, removed afterwards, and no token is in an argument or the environment. Then it deletes the job's own `oby/` branch (`DELETE …/git/refs/heads/<branch>`), never another; one left over doesn't fail the merge. `outgoing` lists `merge` and `branchDelete`, `pullRequest.state` becomes `MERGED`, and `mergedBy` is `{"by": "owner" | "memory", "memory": "<memory id>" | null, "at": "<date>"}` (absent until then). Oby never pushes here, so it force-pushes nothing; it never force-pushes a branch it didn't create.

**Whose approval.** The workflow's `review` says who approves: `oby`, Oby's own review of the very commit, for the owner's own pull requests, merged with the owner's own login (the default); `reviewer`, Oby's reviewer account (an optional second GitHub account, since a pull request's author can't approve it; its login is `github_login::reviewer`'s, and every reviewer login Oby has used is kept for good in `home/github/reviewers.json`, 0600, so one signed out since never passes for a teammate); `teammate`, a teammate's Oby over the Obysphere, for a repository that needs another person's approval (Tetrix's), which approves as its own owner; `person`, someone else with write access, on GitHub. While the merge waits on that approval and on no fixed rule, the follower can ask for it (`merge::wanted_approval`), while the checks still run.

**When it doesn't merge.** Nothing is sent, and obid says why in its own words. Where Oby can't go on by itself, `mergeError` has the reason and `mergeNeedsYou` is true: no workflow yet (`I don't know how obi merges yet, so I left it open.`), a base Oby never merges into (`Its base is main: merge it on GitHub.`) or not the workflow's, a branch or head that isn't the job's own, a scan that wasn't clean or couldn't run, a pull request that isn't the owner's own on Oby's own review, another person's approval GitHub requires that no teammate's Oby can give, a commit that would close an issue as it lands, GitHub's rules still blocking it or a draft, and GitHub refusing the merge (`Not merged: GitHub didn't merge it (…).`). What may pass by itself leaves `mergeError` null: checks pending or red, a review asking for changes or no approval that counts yet, an author GitHub didn't name, a Mac check not passed on the head, a conflict, a branch behind its base, a job not done. Those are the pull request's own rounds and reviewers' (#610).

### obid only: permissions answered without the owner (#586)

A job should never ask the owner about anything that stays inside its sandbox ("The job sandbox", under obid only: Obi-to-Obi review jobs: it writes only to its worktree and temporary folder, has no code-host login, no vault secret and a clone whose push URL is invalid). The owner's decision of 2026-10-17: the agent takes most decisions, and the owner is asked only when it is extremely necessary. So `obid` has a fifth permission mode, **`sandbox`**. It was the default until #1570: a new install (no `permissions.json`, or one with no saved mode) now starts in `bypass`, as Sangam's Settings and the owner's decision of 2026-10-27 say (Settings › Permissions, `GET /v1/settings/permissions`); a mode the owner saved stays, `sandbox` too. The apps no longer offer it: only the owner's API calls set it. The Swift runner doesn't have it and its default stays `bypass`.

| In `sandbox` | |
|---|---|
| **Answered by obid's rules, recorded on the job** | File edits in the worktree; commands and tests (`cargo test`, `npm test`, `git commit`); reads and searches inside the worktree; the agent's own bookkeeping (its to-do list, a helper agent) and the MCP tools of enabled plug-ins, as in every mode. |
| **Refused** (the fixed rules, in every mode) | Pushing or posting (only Publish does), a path outside the worktree, a background subagent, other MCP tools. The agent hears why. |
| **Asked** (a `permission` on the job and a `permission` inbox item, the card saying `why`) | The network beyond the model's API: a web fetch or search, a download or an install (`curl`, `wget`, `ssh`, `npm install`, `cargo add`, `pip install`, `python3 -m pip`, `git clone`, `git fetch`, `docker pull`, a bare `yarn` or `bundle`, `go mod`, `pipx`, `uvx`, `deno`, `gradle`, `mvn`, `rustup`, `swift package resolve`, `npx` or `bunx` of a tool the repository didn't install, …), a service's client (`psql`, `redis-cli`, `kubectl`, `aws`, `gcloud`, `terraform`, …), bash's `/dev/tcp` and `/dev/udp`, and what obid can't read: a program named by a variable or a substitution (`$c`, `$(echo curl)`), inline code (`python3 -c`, `node -e`, `perl -e`, `ruby -e`) and `find -exec`. Secrets, logins and the vault: a well-known secret file or folder by its name (`~/.ssh`, `.aws`, `.kube`, `.docker`, `api-token`, `auth.json`, `.credentials.json`, `.netrc`, `.npmrc`, `.env` but not `.env.example`, and a glob that could match one, like `.cred*`; the keys and patterns of `git config` are not files, only what follows its `-f`, `--file` or `--blob`), the agent's own login (the job's real account folder, its login copy and the links to it in the job's `HOME`, and `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `$CODEX_HOME`, `$CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`), the tokens obid gives a job (`$OBY_MCP_TOKEN`, `$OBY_CHECK_TOKEN`), printing the environment (`printenv` with no name, a bare `env` or `set`, `export -p`, `/proc/*/environ`; `printenv NAME` prints only that variable, as `echo $NAME` does, and asks only for a secret's name), a keychain's passwords (`security find-…`), `gh auth`, or a value the vault keeps, in any form, anywhere in the call. A committed `.env.test` or `.env.development` in the worktree (git has it) is read without asking. A read or search outside the worktree and the job's temporary folder, and a command that names a file outside them and the system's folders (`/usr`, `/etc`, `/opt`, `/dev`, `/System`, `/Library`, …), like `cat /Users/someone/notes.txt` or `ls /tmp`. A tool obid doesn't know stays inside the sandbox, and a Cursor call that gives only a title for its command, or no place for its read. |

- **On macOS the OS holds the network; on Linux the questions are still best-effort.** A job's agent on macOS reaches nothing beyond loopback and no DNS resolver: whatever leaves the Mac goes through obid's egress proxy, which decides each site and asks the owner about a new one ([obid only: the egress proxy](#obid-only-the-egress-proxy-616)). So a program that opens a socket itself, bash's `/dev/tcp`, `python3 -c` or `find -exec curl` gets no further than the proxy lets it, whatever the reading of its command made of it. The reading stays, as the better prompt: it asks before the command runs. On Linux the agent still has slirp4netns, which reaches the whole network and the host's loopback at `10.0.2.2`, so there what reaches the network beyond the model's API is caught only by reading the command, and a program that opens a socket itself isn't caught (#616's next step).
- Matching a command's words is best-effort, like publishing's (obid only: publishing). `python -c` counts as inline code only before `-m` or a script (`python3 -m pytest -c setup.cfg` doesn't), and `find -exec` reaches the network only when what it runs does (`-exec rm {} +` doesn't). obid reads a command the way a shell does: each simple command between `|`, `;`, `&`, `(`, `)` and new lines outside quotes, through the wrappers before it (`sudo`, `timeout 5s`, `env FOO=1`), and the code inside it too: `$(…)` and backticks (also inside double quotes), what `bash -c`, `sh -ec` or `eval` runs, and a here-document or here-string fed to a shell. A quoted argument, any other here-document, a commit's message (`git commit -m`) and the pattern of `grep` or `rg` are words, not commands or files, so a commit whose message says "curl" or "~/.ssh" goes through. Code nested more than four levels deep asks, as a secret does. A script that opens a socket itself isn't caught by its words: on macOS the box and the egress proxy hold it (above), on Linux nothing does yet. A command's network use is asked about before it runs, and the owner's yes covers the sites its addresses name: the proxy lets the agent reach them without a second card.
- **Secrets wait for the owner in every mode** (the re-review of #586). What touches secrets, a login or the vault asks in `ask`, `acceptEdits`, `plan` and `bypass` too, reads included, and no mode or saved rule lets it through: only the owner's own answer to its card does (the brain can't, even once). A waiting secrets card is never settled by a change of mode or a new rule, so neither the brain's `set_mode` nor `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode` answers it. This narrows `bypass` on obid: it answers everything but secrets.
- **Paths are read where the command runs.** A relative word is a path from the worktree, or from where a `cd` (or `pushd`) earlier in the same command line leaves it, with links followed: a committed link to the account's folder (`cat link/settings.json`) is a secret, and `cat ../../x` is outside. A subshell's `cd` (`( … )`, `$(…)`, backticks) ends with the subshell: after `(cd sub)` the folder is the one before. A command's own folder is read like a `cd` for that command: `git -C <dir>` (before git's subcommand), `make -C <dir>` or `--directory=<dir>`, `tar -C <dir>`, `env -C <dir>` or `--chdir=<dir>`; it is outside like any path, and its relative words are paths from there. A folder counts as a system folder only when it is one both as written and after links (on macOS `/home` leads into `/System`). A `cd` from an earlier call isn't known, so `../docs` after a previous call's `cd core` asks.
- **A folder obid can't tell makes the rest of the line `Outside`** (#636). When obid can't tell where a `cd` lands, every later simple command of the same line is outside, whatever it names: `cd -`, `cd $OLDPWD` or any folder named by a variable or a substitution (`cd "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)"`), `popd`, `pushd` with no folder or a place in its stack (`+1`), a bare `cd` when obid doesn't know the job's `HOME`, a `cd` inside code obid reads out of a word (`bash -c`, `eval`, a here-document fed to a shell), and a `cd` in a pipeline or in the background (a shell may run it in a subshell of its own). Likewise a command whose own folder (`git -C "$D"`, above) obid can't tell is outside. The commands before it aren't.
- **git settings that run a program ask** (`Unknown`). A command may give git exactly these, as written, and nothing else: the variables `GIT_EDITOR=true`, `GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR=:`, `GIT_PAGER=cat` and `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` (before the program, or after `env`), and `git -c core.editor=true` and `git -c commit.gpgsign=false` before git's subcommand (#636). Each keeps git from opening an editor, a pager, a prompt or a signing program. Who a commit is by and when (`GIT_AUTHOR_*`, `GIT_COMMITTER_*`, `GIT_PAGER_IN_USE`, `-c user.name=…`, `-c user.email=…`) and colours (`-c color.*`) go through whatever their value: they run no program. Any other `GIT_*` variable (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_PAGER=less`, `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`) and any other `-c` setting (`core.sshCommand`, `core.pager`, `alias.*`, `gpg.program`) ask, as do `--config-env` and `--exec-path=`. `busybox` and `toybox` count as network, like the other multi-call tools.
- **Known gaps of the reading**: programs that run code they're given without a flag obid reads, like `awk` (its `system()`), `lua` and `Rscript`, aren't treated as network: asking for every `awk` would block normal work (on macOS the egress proxy still decides any site they reach). And these ask though they're harmless, by design: a write to `/tmp` (outside the job's folders; the sandbox refuses it anyway), a `sed` whose text names `GITHUB_TOKEN`, and `"$PYTHON" -m pytest` (a program named by a variable). `/Library` stays readable, like the other system folders.
- **The owner's rules still apply**, in this order: a deny rule refuses; a saved allow rule lets through what would ask for the network or a read outside the worktree (the owner's own earlier decision), but **never secrets**: a card about secrets has `rule` and `always` null, so it can only be allowed once. "Always" covers what was asked about, not the whole tool: a fetch saves `WebFetch(domain:<site>)` (that site and its subdomains), a search outside the worktree `Grep(<place>)` (likewise `Glob`, `LS`). A job whose `mode` this obid can't read (one a newer obid wrote) is treated as `ask`; a job from before modes is still `bypass`. Then `sandbox` answers or asks as above. In `plan` and the other modes nothing changes.
- **Standing rules stay confirm cards** (the owner's decision of 2026-10-16). `sandbox` is not a standing rule the brain can create: the brain's plan can't name it (`set_mode` and a dispatch's `mode` take `ask`, `acceptEdits`, `plan`, `bypass`), `bypass`, "always allow" and accepting another Obi's request are `confirm` items as before (obid only: the brain), and the agent's own questions (#448) are asked as before: Claude Code's `AskUserQuestion` waits for the owner in `sandbox` and every other mode (#858, `permission` above). Only the owner's API calls (`PUT /v1/settings/permissions`, `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode`) set `sandbox`.
- **The owner can go back to asking for commands**: set Settings › Permissions to `acceptEdits` (edits go, every command asks) or `ask`. Jobs already dispatched keep their mode.
- **The record.** Every permission obid answers itself in a job of the owner's own, the refused ones too, is kept on the job: `answered`, the latest 50, oldest first, and `answeredCount`, how many obid answered in all (the agent's bookkeeping calls, `TodoWrite`, `Task` and `Agent`, aren't counted; what the owner answers themselves isn't obid's). A waiting prompt that a new mode or a new rule answers is counted when it is answered.

  ```json
  {"answered": [{"id": "p1a2b3", "tool": "Bash", "title": "Run cargo test", "detail": "cargo test --workspace",
                 "decision": "allowed", "at": "2026-10-17T09:12:00Z"},
                {"id": "p4d5e6", "tool": "Bash", "title": "Run git push", "detail": "git push origin HEAD",
                 "decision": "refused", "reason": "obid publishes the job's work itself. …", "at": "2026-10-17T09:12:03Z"}],
   "answeredCount": 2}
  ```

  `decision` is `allowed` or `refused`; `reason` is what the agent heard, only on a refusal; `detail` is cut to 300 characters, with a login in an address (`https://user:pass@host`) and every word that looks like a token (the secret scan's rules) taken out. The `permission` a job waits on may also carry `why`, one sentence saying why it asks when `sandbox` would otherwise have answered ("It reaches the network beyond the model's API.", "It touches secrets or the vault.", "It reads outside the job's worktree."). The Swift runner sends neither key.
- Acceptance, as the API tests show it: a job that edits files, runs tests and installs nothing ends `done` with no inbox item and `answered` listing each; one that tries to push is refused and says so in `answered`; one that installs, fetches a page, reads `~/.ssh` or lists `/etc` waits with a `permission` and `why`.

### obid only: the egress proxy (#616)

On macOS a job's agent has one way off the Mac: an HTTP `CONNECT` proxy obid runs on `127.0.0.1`, on a port it picks at start (only where the job sandbox is Seatbelt's). Its Seatbelt profile denies `network-outbound` to every address but loopback, and the DNS resolver's socket (the proxy names hosts itself), so a socket the agent or anything it runs opens to anywhere else fails at the OS (`a_proxied_agent_reaches_loopback_and_nothing_beyond`: IPv4, IPv6, UDP and a host name lookup all fail inside, while the same probe reaches 1.1.1.1, resolves and sends UDP outside; it fails on the profile without the proxy). The agent gets `HTTPS_PROXY`, `https_proxy`, `HTTP_PROXY` and `http_proxy` set to `http://oby:<token>@127.0.0.1:<port>`, `NO_PROXY` and `no_proxy` set to `localhost,127.0.0.1,::1` (obid's MCP servers and the job broker, a test's own servers), and `NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1`, so Node's own `fetch` and `https` use it too; these win over a proxy in obid's own environment. The token is the job worker's own, made when its agent first starts and learned as a secret (masked wherever it's repeated); it, and everything decided for it, goes when the worker ends, and it's never in an argument or a log line. A request without it, or with a token no worker holds, gets `407 Proxy Authentication Required` and nobody is asked. Only tunnels: a plain `http://` request gets `405` (use https).

**A tunnel carries TLS for the host it was opened for, and nothing else** (#1471's review). A CDN's edge serves whichever of its sites the TLS hello names, whatever `CONNECT` named: `chatgpt.com`'s and `registry.npmjs.org`'s addresses serve `developers.cloudflare.com`, `pypi.org`'s `www.python.org`. So after `200` the proxy reads the client's whole ClientHello (its records reassembled, at most 64 KiB, within 15 seconds) and sends it on only when its `server_name` is the `CONNECT` host (case and a final dot aside), or, for an IP address, when it names none; a hello with `encrypted_client_hello`, one naming another host, two names or any extension twice, no name for a host, or anything that isn't TLS closes the tunnel before a byte reaches the site.

Each site (the host, and `:<port>` but for 443) is decided once for the job's worker, in this order:

1. **The tool's own services, on 443**, go through, unrecorded: Claude Code's `anthropic.com`, `claude.ai` and `claude.com`; Codex's `openai.com` and `chatgpt.com`; Cursor's `cursor.sh` and `cursor.com`; each with its subdomains (their model APIs, and the settings and feature flags the CLIs ask their vendor for).
2. **Telemetry to anyone else** (`datadoghq.com`, `sentry.io`, and their subdomains: Claude Code sends Datadog its logs) is refused, without asking.
3. **Another Obi's review job** reaches nothing more: it only reads, and never asks.
4. **In Sandbox mode, a package registry** goes through on 443, recorded in `answered`: `registry.npmjs.org`, `registry.yarnpkg.com`, `repo.yarnpkg.com`, `repo.maven.apache.org`, `repo1.maven.org`, `plugins.gradle.org`, `services.gradle.org`, `cdn.cocoapods.org`; exactly these hosts. They are only registries whose edge answers just the host the TLS hello named (Cloudflare's: each answered `403` to a hello naming it with `Host` naming another site of its edge, 2026-10-26). `pypi.org`, `files.pythonhosted.org`, `crates.io`, `index.crates.io`, `static.crates.io`, `static.rust-lang.org`, `rubygems.org` and `index.rubygems.org` (Fastly's), and `proxy.golang.org` and `sum.golang.org` (Google's), were on the list until 2026-10-26 and aren't (`ROUTED_BY_HOST`): their edges answered such a request with the other site, and the proxy doesn't read the `Host` inside a tunnel. They are decided as any other site (5., below), so `pip install`, `cargo fetch`, `gem install` and `go mod download` in Sandbox mode wait for the owner's card, or a `WebFetch(domain:…)` rule, the first time in a job (`only_registries_whose_edge_answers_its_own_host_go_through_by_default`).
5. **Any other site is a fetch from it** (`WebFetch` of `https://<site>/`), answered as a fetch the agent asked for: the repository's rules, then the job's mode (`bypass` lets it through), else the owner's `permission` card, "Fetch <site>", `why` "It reaches the network beyond the model's API.", its `detail` ending "Allowing it lets this job reach <site> until it ends.", with Allow, Always allow (which saves `WebFetch(domain:<site>)` for the repository) and Deny. A `WebFetch(domain:<site>)` deny covers the site and its subdomains on every port; an allow, the port it names (none: 443). Allow lets every later connection to that site through until the worker ends, Deny refuses them, without another card. Every connection to a site being asked about waits for that one card, at most 5 minutes (then `403`, and the card keeps waiting: its answer holds for the next); at most 3 of a job's sites wait for the owner at once (counted as the card is put), and a 4th is refused this once (`403`, "Other sites are waiting for the owner"), with no card. A deny rule is checked at every connection, so one saved later stops a site the owner had let through.

What a mode or the rules decided is decided again when the job's mode changes (`set_mode`, `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode`) or a rule is saved or forgotten (for every job), a waiting card such a change settled included; the owner's own answers to cards stay. Only the owner's own yes to a call's card opens sites besides: the `http(s)://` addresses of a fetch, and of the commands in it that reach the network as obid reads them (`curl https://…`, `git clone https://…`), until the worker ends, and its card says which (`detail`, as above). A comment (`git fetch origin # https://…`), an `echo` and a saved rule's or a mode's allow open nothing: the proxy decides those sites itself.

A connection has 5 seconds to send its request, and at most 128 wait to (another is closed at once); one whose token checks out takes one of 1,024 places every job's tunnels share (none left: `503`), and at most 64 of a job's tunnels are open at once (another gets `429`), so no job can hold the places another's model calls need; obid raises its own limit of open files to what macOS allows (10,240) at start, since each tunnel holds two. A tunnel nothing went through for 10 minutes ends. A refused connection gets `403` with the reason as its text, a site that can't be reached `502`. Refusals are logged at debug, never at info. The proxy reaches a site through the proxy obid itself is behind, when there is one: obid's own `HTTPS_PROXY`, an `http://` one (its login percent-decoded, `NO_PROXY`'s domains straight, `NO_PROXY=*` every host; another scheme, `https://` or `socks5://`, is said once in the log and the proxy connects straight), else the Mac's https proxy (`scutil --proxy`: `HTTPSProxy`, `HTTPSPort`, the `ExceptionsList`'s domains straight), read again after a minute; loopback always goes straight. Codex runs with `-c features.plugins=false`: at every start it clones its curated plug-ins from github.com with `git`, which would ask, and a job's `CODEX_HOME` has none installed. `the_egress_proxy_lets_through_asks_and_refuses_by_site` shows the rule's site tunnelled with a hello naming it and closed for another name, an encrypted hello or no TLS; no token refused; a deny on its port and another; one card for two connections; Deny and Allow each holding; the 4th site refused while 3 wait; the tool's API on 443 only, a registry and telemetry; a site the owner allowed a fetch from going through; a comment's and a rule's allow opening nothing; Bypass's decisions decided again in Sandbox mode; and the token dead once the worker ends.

Checked on this Mac on 2026-10-26, each CLI as obid starts it in a job, in the box, with a made-up login in a scratch home: Claude Code 2.1.302 reached `api.anthropic.com` through the proxy, Codex 0.162.1 `api.openai.com` and `chatgpt.com`, cursor-agent 2026.10.01 `api2.cursor.sh`, each sending the proxy's login and a TLS hello naming the host, and each heard its API refuse the made-up login. Without the proxy's variables, under a profile that denies the same, Codex only says it's waiting for the network.

What it doesn't hold back:

- **Loopback, and what listens.** A test's own servers, obid's MCP servers and the proxy are there, so the agent reaches the person's own services on `localhost` too, as before: one that sends things on (a local HTTP or SOCKS proxy, a DNS forwarder, a database linked elsewhere) is a way out. And a server the agent starts on `0.0.0.0` serves the network: Seatbelt's only hosts are `*` and `localhost`, its `localhost` takes a listen on `0.0.0.0` too, and its inbound rules hold only at the listen, so refusing it would refuse a test's own server on 127.0.0.1 as well (tried 2026-10-26). macOS's firewall (System Settings › Network › Firewall) is what holds it.
- **What an allowed site is sent.** The proxy sees names, not what goes inside the tunnel (no TLS inspection). A tool's vendor and the default registries take uploads (`npm publish`, `cargo publish`, `gem push` go to the same hosts) with a login the agent brings, a token planted in a repository, say.
- **The `Host` inside a tunnel.** The TLS name is checked; the HTTP `Host` inside the tunnel isn't read (no TLS inspection). Some edges pick the site they serve by `Host`, not by the hello's name: Fastly's and Google's did (2026-10-26), Cloudflare's, Vercel's, CloudFront's and Cursor's own answered `403`, `404` or `421`. So the default registries are only Cloudflare's (4., above), and every tool's own service tried answered so (Anthropic's, Claude's, OpenAI's and ChatGPT's on Cloudflare, `cursor.com` on Vercel, `api2.cursor.sh`, the status pages on CloudFront); those are a sample of the subdomains each tool's domains cover, not all of them. A site the owner allows on a card, or a rule or Bypass allows, is tunnelled as it is: when its edge picks by `Host`, the job reaches that edge's other sites too.
- **An allowed site that doesn't speak TLS.** Only TLS goes through a tunnel, so Postgres, ssh or plain HTTP to a site the owner allowed fails all the same.
- **The system's daemons that fetch for a caller**, like trustd's certificate checks and nsurlsessiond's background downloads: they're outside the profile.
- **The Apple check.** `oby-check apple` has a Mac that follows the runner build and test the job's commit with Xcode (Apple check, above), outside this box and with the network: a job's tests run there. It's off by default since the launch build.
- **Linux.** Not yet, and nothing above changes there (the registries' list included): the agent keeps slirp4netns, the whole network and the host's loopback at `10.0.2.2`, and gets no proxy variables. The next step: no slirp4netns, the agent's namespace with its own loopback only, and a forwarder in it from a loopback port to the proxy over a Unix socket bound into the box; the proxy then refuses a name that resolves to the host's loopback.
- **The brain, a conversation's agent and a Sphere answering run** keep the network as before; a conversation's shell command gets the network only when the owner gave it to that command, and then all of it.

### obid only: publishing the owner's work

`obid` publishes as [Job](#job) and [`GET`](#get-v1jobsidpublish) and [`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish`](#post-v1jobsidpublish) say, by itself when a job ends `done` with commits made in that run, and by hand with a registered device's signature. What is particular to `obid`:

- **Where it runs.** Every git command of publishing runs in `obid`'s own clone (`home/clones/<owner>/<name>`), on the commit the job's branch is at there (`obid` took the job's commits into the clone and moved the branch), never in the job's worktree, whose git files the agent could write. Objects are read as they are (`--no-replace-objects`), no hook runs, and every diff the scan reads is `--no-ext-diff --no-textconv --text`, root commits included (`--root`). What the scan reads, it reads with the clone's own configuration only (`GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null`): a setting of the person's (`log.showRoot = false`, an attributes file) can't narrow it. The fetch and the push keep the person's git configuration (their remotes' addresses and sign-in).
- **Where it goes.** The push goes to the repository's `remote` (obid only: configuration) by its address, not to `origin`, whose push URL stays invalid; it pushes one commit, `--no-follow-tags --no-signed --recurse-submodules=no`, and `--force-with-lease` only for a rebase of its own `oby/` branch over the commit it pushed there last. A remote whose address carries a login (`https://user:token@…`) is refused (`publishError` says so), since the address would be in git's arguments. For an `https` remote, git signs in with the owner's `gh` as its credential helper (`gh auth git-credential`: the credential travels on a pipe, never in an argument or the environment). Clones and fetches of an `https` remote sign in the same way, git's own helpers reset first, so a private repository clones with nothing set up but gh's own sign-in (no `gh auth setup-git`), and gh's token never reaches another helper (after a clone git asks every helper to `store` it, and Apple's git turns `osxkeychain` on system-wide). Without a gh, a clone uses git's own settings.
- **GitHub.** `obid` has no GitHub app: the pull request is opened (`gh pr create`, ready for review, never a draft), found (`gh pr list --head`), brought up to date (`gh pr edit --body-file`) and followed (`gh pr view`) with the owner's own `gh` login: the `gh` Oby.app carries (`Contents/MacOS/gh`, pinned and signed with the app, apps/mac/app/Tools/bundle-gh.sh) when obid runs from inside the app, else the one on the login shell's `PATH` (`GET /v1/status` says `"github": "owner"` and `"checks": "no app"`). `gh` runs with `obid`'s environment and `GH_PROMPT_DISABLED=1`; its description goes in a 0600 file in `home/tmp/publish-<job id>/`, removed afterwards. Only a pull request URL of the job's own repository, `https://github.com/<repo>/pull/<n>`, is kept or followed. An open pull request is taken as the job's only when it comes from the repository itself (`isCrossRepository` false: a fork's branch of the same name is someone else's), into the job's base, and its head is the commit obid just pushed, or the one it pushed before; otherwise obid opens its own. Without `gh`, `publishError` says the pull request couldn't be opened; a push that went out before (one to an `ssh` or `file` remote needs no `gh`) is listed in `outgoing` and counted in `pushed`. Only a remote on GitHub (`https://github.com/…`, `ssh://git@github.com/…` or `git@github.com:…`) gets a pull request, unless the repository's `pullRequests` says otherwise: for any other, nothing is asked of `gh` (no pull request, no issue read), and the push is the whole publish (#627). `POST` then answers `{"url": null}`.
- **The base** is fetched from the remote first and pinned to the commit it is at then: what the push sends beyond it is what the scan reads. Jobs on one repository that end together all publish (#628): obid's clone sets `fetch.hideRefs` to `worktrees/`, so a fetch doesn't check what it got against the HEAD of another job's worktree, whose commits stay in that job's own object folder until obid keeps them (git said `did not send all necessary objects` otherwise); one fetch runs at a time per clone, no job's commits land in the clone while one runs, and a fetch that still gets that answer is tried once more. When it can't be fetched, nothing goes out (`publishError` `Not published: couldn't fetch <base>: …`, git's own line, never its command line or the remote's address).
- **The scan** is `crate::scan`'s, on everything that goes out: each commit's patch (`git log -p --diff-merges=remerge`), its added lines (named by the diff's `+++` line, which names a file exactly), with gitleaks on them; every name a commit adds at a path its parent didn't have (each file's, folder's and submodule's own name, from `git log --raw -z --diff-merges=separate --ignore-submodules=none`, so never split or quoted, a merge read against each parent and a submodule never hidden, whatever the clone's settings; a name already on the base at the same path is the base's), with the built-in rules reading a name as they read a line, and gitleaks on them, one name per line (a newline in a name written as `\n`), a finding it can't place in a name counting all the same; a file whose name alone holds secrets (`.env`, a private key's file) counts only as a file, not a folder named so; every file those commits send, read whole, for the secrets `obid` holds (its API token, its Obi key, the Sphere's delegation and token, and the vault's: [obid only: the vault](#obid-only-the-vault)), in every form `crate::secrets` knows, a file over 32 MiB being a finding; each commit as git keeps it (`cat-file`), its author and committer (`commit author`) and its message's own bytes (`commit message`); and the branch's name, the pull request's title and description, and each of the agent's summaries as it wrote them (`agent's summary`: the description's quote of it has invisible characters in it, which a known secret holding `@x` or `#1` would no longer match). A commit whose headers aren't exactly what git writes for a plain commit (one `tree`, its `parent`s, one `author`, one `committer`, in UTF-8: no `encoding`, which would re-encode its message, no signature, no other header) or whose message isn't UTF-8 text is refused: nothing goes out, and `publishError` names the commit, never what it holds. A name that holds a possible secret is never repeated: wherever `obid` says where it found something (`publishError`, the summary, the log, the API), that file is `a file whose name obid won't show`, and the line adds that it is among the names of the files and folders the branch adds to its base, which is where the owner looks. A possible secret in `commit author`, or in a file obid won't name, gets no fix round. A file finding names the commit whose patch introduced it, and whether the branch's tip still has it. A gitleaks that can't run is a scan that couldn't run: nothing goes out, and the job stays `done` with `publishError` `Not published: the secret scan couldn't run (…)`. So is no gitleaks at all (#1026): the built-in rules alone miss keys only gitleaks knows (Google's `AIza…`, npm, Hugging Face, Twilio, SendGrid tokens, JWTs), so they aren't the scan, and every outgoing path that scans (publishing, merges, memories, alerts, Tetrix) sends nothing, saying `gitleaks isn't installed: Oby.app carries its own; elsewhere, put gitleaks on obid's PATH`. obid takes the gitleaks Oby.app carries (`Contents/MacOS/gitleaks`: gitleaks 8.30.1, universal, checked against its SHA-256 and signed with the app when it's built, apps/mac/app/Tools/bundle-gitleaks.sh), else the login shell's. gitleaks always reads a fixed first line of obid's (`obid`, no word a rule looks for) before the lines it's given: what starts like a PDF, a zip or a gzip (`%PDF-1.4`, `PK\x03\x04`) is something gitleaks skips whole, so a diff whose first added line looked like one would otherwise be scanned as nothing. A finding wholly on that line doesn't count; one that runs past it (its `EndLine` 2 or more) does, on the first line given. gitleaks also sniffs further in (`ustar` at byte 257 for a tar, `CD001` at byte 32769 for an ISO image) and then reads nothing at all, whatever comes first, so obid puts a canary last: a made-up GitHub token, new every run and never kept, in the shape gitleaks' `github-pat` rule always flags, after another `obid` line, so a last line that only names a secret (`API_KEY=`, `token:`) isn't read on into it. A report without the canary (gitleaks exited 0, or wrote no report it could read) is a scan that couldn't run, `gitleaks didn't read its input`, and nothing goes out; the canary's own finding, and any wholly on that `obid` line, are dropped. gitleaks runs with `--ignore-gitleaks-allow`: a `gitleaks:allow` comment in what's scanned hides nothing (without it, gitleaks drops every finding in the part of its input that holds one, and past about 300 KB the canary is in another part, so the scan would read clean). The Swift engine's own gitleaks call has none of this yet (#657).
- **The pull request's description** quotes each of the agent's summaries as Markdown, under a label saying they are the agent's words; an invisible character (U+200B) after each `<`, `&`, `@`, `[` and `]`, after a `#` before a letter or digit or one that starts a heading, and in each closing keyword and heading underline, keeps any of it from acting (no HTML or open `<!--` comment, entity such as `&commat;` or `&#x23;` that reads as `@` or `#`, link, image, alert, task box, mention or closed issue). Code gets the same characters: a fence closed by an indented line or a code span across lines is easy to misread, and one missed would leave the rest of the summary live. It ends with `by Oby: Oby opened this pull request after the check passed and the secret scan was clean. Oby merges it only when its owner says so.` in place of the Swift runner's merge sentence (by the workflow the owner taught it, or on their tap: obid only, merging); a job with no check says `- Check: none, the job had no check.` and ends `by Oby: Oby opened this pull request after the secret scan was clean. Oby merges it only when its owner says so.`
- **Following** reads a published pull request when `obid` starts, right after it publishes one, and every three minutes while it is open; a read or a try in the last three minutes isn't repeated. Each read carries the job on (obid only: merging).
- **By hand.** The device's key is the one it registered (`POST /v1/devices`); each approval is let through once, and what was used is kept for 10 minutes in `home/approvals.json` (0600), so a restart opens no replay. An approval is used up when it is checked, even when publishing then fails. The sentence names the branch, not its commits, so an approval publishes only the run that was approved: when the job's latest run ended after the device signed (`signedAt`), or when `obid` described an earlier run of it (`GET`) in the two minutes before the device signed, the approval could be of either, and `POST` answers `409` `The job ran again since obid said what publishing it would do. Approve it again in two minutes.` What `obid` described, and when, is kept with the job, across restarts. This is a narrowing, not a seal: signing the commit itself is #553. While a publish by hand runs, the job takes no words (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`, `422`) and can't be dismissed (`409`). The refusals are `403`: `That device isn't registered with this runner.`, `The signature doesn't match the device's key.`, `The device approved something else. Approve it again.`, `The approval is more than two minutes old. Approve it again.` and `That approval was already used. Approve it again.`. A publish that fails answers `409` with its line.
- **The journal.** `obid`'s log has one line per thing it sent (`job <id>: sent push <repo> <branch> <commit>`), ids only, and one line per publish that didn't go out, saying why (never a secret).

### obid only: failures

`obid` keeps [Failure records](#failure-record) and serves [`GET /v1/failures`](#get-v1failureslimit50), [`GET /v1/failures/{id}`](#get-v1failuresid) and [`POST /v1/failures/{id}/fix`](#post-v1failuresidfix) with every key above (#445). What is particular to `obid`:

- **What it records.** Each time a job changes, `obid` looks at it as the Swift runner does (Engine/Failures.swift, `found`): a `failed` job is `limit` when it stopped at a limit, `publishError` when its summary starts `Not published:`, and `failed` otherwise; an `interrupted` one, which needs you, is `limit` when it stopped at a limit, `publishError` when its `reason` starts `Not published:`, and `failed` otherwise; a `done` job with `publishError` set is `publishError`; a `failed` job obid's own bug ended, or an `interrupted` one obid kept going down with (below), is `crash` (#911). Only while it is news (it ended within a day), and once: the record's id is `<job>-<trigger>-<when it ended, in seconds>`, and a record is never written again. `obid` looks only as a job changes: it has no sweep, and doesn't look at ended jobs again when it starts, so a failure is missed if `obid` stops between keeping the job and writing its record (#571). A job the owner stopped isn't a failure, and a `done` one with a `reason` (nothing committed, a review that committed) isn't either. The other triggers need what `obid` doesn't run (Oby's review, merging, the minute's sweep, the brain's fallback) and are never recorded: `reviewCap`, `noVerdict`, `noReviewer`, `checkError`, `unnamedFailure`, `mergeError`, `flakyTest`, `stuck`, `brainFallback`, and the interventions `handMerge` and `outsideCommit` (a person merging is how `obid` works, never an intervention). `intervention` is always false.
- **obid's own bugs (#914, #911).** A panic in a job's task ends that job, never its slot: it's `failed`, its `reason` and `summary` `obid hit a bug while running this job, so it stopped it: <the panic's first line, at most 300 characters, every secret obid knows taken out>. Its branch and worktree are kept. Try again runs it again.`, its slot is given back first (so a queued job starts in it, whatever happens next), then its login's copy, its agent's MCP configuration, what it waited on and any claim on publishing it go; its agent's whole process group ends, and so does its check's; another Obi's review answers its exchange, failed. The other jobs keep running. Every panic's line in obid's log (its stderr) is `obid: panic at <file:line>: <message>`, the message with every secret obid knows taken out, never Rust's raw line; the hook waits on no lock and refreshes nothing, so when the secrets can't be read at once the message is withheld. The owner gets one `failed` inbox item about it (one per such end, made once across restarts): its `text` the reason's first line, `options` `Try again` (runs it again as its Try again does, a fresh run when it had no session) and `Dismiss` (dismisses the job). Another Obi's review can't be run again here: its reason ends `The Obi that asked for this review was told it failed.` and its item offers only `Dismiss`. A panic after the job had ended (in what comes after its run) leaves it as it ended, the bug noted in its `changes` (`obid hit a bug after this ended: …`). A publish's claim on the job goes however it ends, a panic or a Publish by hand whose request went included. The event orchestrator leaves such a job, its item and its Mac checks to the owner: tried again by Oby, a bug that's always there would only loop.
- **Where.** One file per record, `home/failures/<id>.json` (0600, in a 0700 folder), never pruned. `GET /v1/failures/{id}` takes only ids `obid` makes (letters, digits and dashes): anything else is `404`, like an id it doesn't have (`There's no failure <id>.`). `limit` is a whole number (`422` `limit is a number.` otherwise, as `GET /v1/jobs`), below 0 is none and above 1,000 is 1,000.
- **The evidence**, with every secret `obid` knows taken out (its API token, its Obi key, the Sphere's delegation and token, and the vault's once it is ported, #457: `crate::secrets`), and so are `title` and `what` (a limit names the check's command): `summary` is the end of the job's report (4,000 characters), `step` its stage, phase, step and round, `check` the end of what its last check printed (`home/logs/<job>/check.log`, 3,000 characters) and `Publish: <publishError>`, or null; a `check.log` written before a failure of the job `obid` recorded already is an earlier run's and isn't quoted. `reviews` and `log` are always empty (`obid` runs no review of its own, and keeps no log lines in memory), `worktree` and `source` always null (both are read at the post-mortem).
- **No post-mortem yet.** `obid` runs no post-mortem turn, so `postMortem`, `studyError`, `issue`, `issueError`, `fix`, `fixedBy` and `fixedAt` are null, `tries` is 0 and `acted` false. With nothing studied, nothing is acted on: no `What went wrong:` paragraph in the job's report, no `oby-failure` issue filed, updated or closed on GitHub, no `failure` inbox item, and `POST /v1/failures/{id}/fix` answers `409` `Oby hasn't studied this failure yet.` for every record it has (`404` for one it doesn't). When the post-mortem comes, filing its issue is an outgoing action: it goes through the publish gate (the secret scan on the title, the body and the arguments; nothing goes out if the scanner can't run) with the owner's `gh`, as publishing does. The post-mortem, acting on it and the fix job are #571.

### obid only: configuration

```sh
obid serve --config /path/to/config.json
```

```json
{"listen": "127.0.0.1:7811",
 "home": "/abs/path/obid-home",
 "owner": "wellington",
 "agents": {"claude": {"bin": "claude", "accounts": [{"id": "default", "name": "Default", "configDir": null}]}},
 "repos": [{"repo": "obi-e2e/fixture-app", "remote": "file:///abs/path/remote.git", "defaultBranch": "main", "check": "node --test"}],
 "allowFileRemotes": false,
 "web": "/abs/path/obi/apps/web/dist",
 "telemetry": false}
```

- `listen`: an address on 127.0.0.1. obid binds only that, and refuses with `403` every request whose `Host` isn't `127.0.0.1`, `localhost` or `[::1]` (with or without a port), as a Mac runner does. Required, but on macOS, where it is `127.0.0.1:7777` when left out: where the Mac app looks for the runner on the same Mac.
- `home` (required, absolute): everything obid keeps, and the only place it writes besides its `HOME` and `TMPDIR`: its database, the API token (`api-token`, 0600), the accounts the API changed (`accounts.json`, 0600, and the folders it makes, `accounts/<id>`), the Obi key (`keys/`, 0600), clones (`clones/<owner>/<name>`), job worktrees (`worktrees/<job id>`), plug-ins (`plugins/`, `plugin-data/`), and the settings the API changes: `settings.json` (`maxRunning`, `PUT /v1/settings/jobs`; `noCheck`, the repositories the owner said No check for, Job; an older obid's `checksOffered` isn't read) and `permissions.json` (the permission settings and the saved rules: `PUT /v1/settings/permissions`, `/v1/permissions/rules`), each kept across restarts.
- `owner` (required): the owner's id, as their Sphere knows them.
- `agents.claude.bin`: the Claude Code adapter's binary, a path or a command name found on the login shell's `PATH` (AGENTS.md, "PATH"), then in `~/.local/bin`, `~/.bun/bin`, nvm's default node's `bin` (the newest `~/.nvm/versions/node/v*` that `~/.nvm/alias/default` names) and `/opt/homebrew/bin`, where installers put the CLIs that only an interactive shell's `.zshrc` adds to `PATH`. Every process obid starts gets that `PATH`. `accounts`: each `{id, name, configDir, payer}` (`payer` optional); they are the seed the accounts API changes ([obid only: accounts](#obid-only-accounts)); `configDir` is the account's folder, where its login is kept (and, on macOS, what names its Keychain item), or null for the tool's own default login; no box ever gets the folder itself: every run gets a folder of its own with a copy of the login ("The job sandbox", #655). `GET /v1/status` sends each one with `signedIn` true when its login is there and a run can use it (#626): Claude Code's `.credentials.json` in its `configDir`, or `~/.claude/.credentials.json` when `configDir` is null; Codex's `auth.json` in its `configDir`, or `~/.codex/auth.json`, unless its access token ends within 5 minutes (then `signedIn` false and `label` `Run codex once`: a run's copy can't refresh it); Cursor's login file (`.cursor/auth.json` on macOS, `.config/cursor/auth.json` elsewhere) in its `configDir`, or in the person's home for Cursor's own login, holding an `accessToken` or `apiKey`, or for Cursor's own login with no such file, its macOS Keychain items with `cursorKeychain`; an empty value is none, and an access token whose JWT `exp` has passed counts only with an `apiKey` beside it. The Keychain's values are read for this at most once a minute (whether an item exists, every time); one that has ended reads `signedIn: false` with `label` `Run cursor-agent once`, and a job on it doesn't start, saying the Mac's Cursor login has ended (only the Mac's own cursor-agent refreshes it). Each is a regular file, never a link. obid only looks whether Claude Code's exists; Codex's and Cursor's it reads for those fields alone, and sends nothing of them anywhere. A Claude Code login only in the macOS Keychain counts when `claudeKeychain` is on, since obid then copies it where a run reads it (The job sandbox, under obid only: Obi-to-Obi review jobs); with it off it doesn't, since the job sandbox denies the Keychain. Routing (a job with no `account`, the brain's dispatches and reviews) takes the first signed-in account of the tools in order (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor), so with `agent` left out a job goes to Codex when no Claude Code account is signed in; when none is, it takes the first account as before, and the tool's own words say why it can't run. A job naming its account runs there. Settings › Connectors in the web app shows such an account as not checked, never as connected. Codex and Cursor take the same shape when their adapters land (`CODEX_HOME`, `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`).
- `repos`: the repositories jobs may work in, besides GitHub's that gh can push to; the first job in one of those adds it here ([obid only: repositories](#obid-only-repositories)). `remote` is an `https://` or `ssh` URL, or a `file://` URL only when `allowFileRemotes` is true (the e2e test's fixture). `check` is the one-line command that decides `tests_pass` and serves as the job's check: obid runs it with `/bin/sh -c` in the job's worktree, after the agent's round, in the job sandbox with no network but, for the owner's own jobs run here, loopback ("The job sandbox"), and one check at a time, whatever the job (a check waits for the one running, with its step `Waiting for another job's check to finish`; its 20 minutes start when it does, and a stop ends the wait), and a fix, build or test job in a repository with a check gets 3 rounds when it names none (#958). A repository that came from GitHub's list has no `check` until the owner keeps the one Oby asks about (Job, #1062) or adds one to its entry here; obid reads this file when it starts, so restart it after (on a Mac with Oby, the configuration is `~/Library/Application Support/Oby/obid/config.json` and obid is the login item `com.deskree.oby.obid`: `launchctl kickstart -k gui/$(id -u)/com.deskree.oby.obid`). The check is the branch's own command, as a repository's `npm test` is, so a change to it shows in the diff the review reads. **This repository's** is `scripts/oby-check.sh`, which runs only the suites the job's change reaches, then gitleaks, with 4 cores and 4 test threads (its header lists them; `OBY_CHECK_PLAN=1` prints what it would run): `{"repo": "obi-ai-bot/oby", "remote": "https://github.com/obi-ai-bot/oby.git", "defaultBranch": "dev", "check": "scripts/oby-check.sh"}`. `pullRequests` (optional): whether its pushes get a pull request on GitHub with the owner's `gh`; left out, only when `remote` is GitHub's (obid only: publishing, "GitHub"). obid's own tests set it for the fixture's `file://` remote, with a stand-in `gh` (#627).
- `bundledPlugins`: retired with plug-ins (2026-10-27): ignored with a warning.
- `telemetry`: false unless the owner turns it on. obid sends nothing to anyone otherwise.
- `council`: false unless the owner turns it on: then obid may convene a Council review panel (`POST /v1/obid/sphere/council`, [obid only: Obi-to-Obi exchanges](#obid-only-obi-to-obi-exchanges)). It has no UI.
- `vault` (optional): where the owner's vault is ([obid only: the vault](#obid-only-the-vault)): `{"store": "file"}` for `home/vault/secrets.json`, or on macOS `{"store": "keychain"}` for the Swift runner's Keychain items, with an optional `"service"` in place of `com.deskree.oby.runner.vault` (tests use a throwaway one). Left out, it is the Keychain when `home` is the Mac app's runner home on macOS (compared with links resolved and letter case ignored) and the login Keychain file (`~/Library/Keychains/login.keychain-db`, by the user database's home) is there, and the file otherwise. `"keychain"` anywhere but macOS is refused, and so is a blank `service` or one with spaces around it.
- `turnBudget`: how long one conversation turn may plan and act, in seconds, 1 to 110; 110 when left out (obid only: the brain).
- `stallAfter` (obid only): how long a job may go without progress before it stalls (`limit`, above), in seconds, 1 to 86,400; 900 when left out.
- `parkAfter` (obid only): how long a job's agent may wait for the owner's answer before obid parks the job (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/permission`, Parked), in seconds, 1 to 86,400; 600 when left out.
- `jobCeilingHours` (obid only): the safety ceiling of a job's running time, every run added up, against runaway cost only (`limit`, above), in hours, 1 to 168; 12 when left out.
- `jobTokenBudget` (obid only): the safety budget of a job's tokens, cache reads left out (`limit`, above), 1 to 10,000,000,000; 50,000,000 when left out.
